2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
⚫ 上周三美國公布 8 月民間就業人數僅增加 3.8 萬人，低於市場預期的 4.8 萬人。
教育與醫療服務業新增 4.5 萬人，但製造業減少 1.7 萬人，專業與商業服務業
也出現萎縮 。 這反映出民間部門招聘動能放緩 ， 尤其是製造業與專業服務業的疲
弱。美元指數至高點回落 ， 惟上周五美國公布8 月非農就業人數新增 16.2 萬人 ，
遠超預期的 5.6 萬人 ，失業率維持在4.1%；勞動參與率回升至 61.6%， 平均工時
延長至 34.4 小時 ， 顯示勞動市場的韌性 。 雖然薪資增幅僅3.1%， 通膨壓力有限 ，
但強勁的就業數據仍強化了市場對美聯儲加息的預期 。 芝商所 FedWatch 工具顯
示，9 月加息機率已升至 62%，美元指數止跌回穩。
⚫ 紐西蘭央行上週三將官方隔夜拆款利率調升 25bps 至 2.75%， 並釋出緩步緊縮訊
號。央行聲明指出，逐步撤回貨幣政策刺激有助於通膨回到 2%目標中點，同時

降低未來大幅升息的必要性 。 總裁佈雷曼強調 ， 升息時機仍高度不確定 ， 短期核
心通膨可能續升 ， 但逐步退出寬鬆政策仍屬合宜 。 央行亦提醒經濟存在顯著下行
風險，政策將保持漸；然而消息公布後紐幣大跌，主因 2027 年利率預測較原先
預期低。
⚫ 加拿大央行上週三維持政策利率在 2.25%不變 ， 但警告通膨風險升高 。 美國新關
稅增加經濟不確定性 ， 或使企業延後投資與招聘 。 總裁麥克勒姆表示 ， 關稅對整
體經濟影響有限 ， 但中東戰事與高油價可能推升物價壓力 。 央行強調需警惕外部
因素對通膨的擴散效應，加拿大幣止跌回穩。

### Page 3

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
美元兌新台幣匯率走勢
Sep/7/2026
＜上週走勢＞
上週台幣匯價高點為 31.590，低點在 31.827，以 31.630 作收，較前一週收盤價
貶值 0.2 分。
上週台幣先貶後升，週初，聯準會主席華許於全球央行年會發表的鷹派言論強
化市場對聯準會升息的預期，引發台灣市場股匯雙殺，外資匯出令台幣走貶；週二
早盤由於台股走升外資匯入，台幣一度走升，惟逢低進口商買美金力道強勁，加以
投信亦匯出，台幣由升轉貶，不過逢高出口商拋匯，台幣貶幅收斂；週三、周四，
中東戰事升溫，帶動美元指數走升，外資連續兩日賣超台股共計1390 億，匯出力道
強勁，台幣貶至周內低點 31.827，惟出口商拋匯需求及央行進場調節，抑制台幣貶
值空間；臨近周末，受益於美元指數走跌、亞幣走升，台幣止跌回穩，加以外資轉
向匯入，台幣收復上周大部分跌幅，終場在央行調節之下收在 31.630。
＜未來分析＞
預估本週台幣區間震盪。就國際市場而言，美國勞動力市場尚屬穩健，非農就

業人數遠超預期，勞動參與率回升，令市場對於聯準會升息的預期有所回溫，將給
予美元指數支撐；就台灣市場來看，台灣 8 月製造業 PMI 54.7，顯示台灣製造業景
氣續呈大幅擴張，新增訂單持續暢旺，有利台幣表現，惟上週外資賣超台股並於外
匯市場匯出，將為台幣帶來貶值的壓力。綜合以上，預估本週台幣走勢區間震盪。
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.500-31.850

### Page 4

4  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌人民幣匯率走勢  
Sep/7/2026 
＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週人民幣震盪走升。週初，儘管華許鷹派言論提振美元指數，然而中國出口
商逢匯價反彈結售美金，帶動人民幣震盪走升，惟人民幣中間價升值速度放緩，顯
示監管單位在地緣政治風險再起的情況下，允許人民幣升值的意願有所降低，抑制
人民幣升值速度；周間由於人民幣已走升至年內高點附近，升值空間有限，日內人
民幣整體維持高檔窄幅盤整的格局 ， 週三晚間美國公布ADP 就業人數低於預期 ， 美
元指數走弱，人民幣受益走升；臨近周末人民幣開出中間價格為 6.7787，升破 6.78
整數大關並創三年半新高，加以日幣延續升值的態勢，同為亞幣的人民幣亦持續走
升，終場境外人民幣收在 6.7048，週線升值 264 點，境內人民幣則收在 6.7108，週
線升值 161 點。  
＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週人民幣區間偏升。國際市場方面，美國非農就業人數遠高預期，顯示
美國勞動力市場仍具韌性，加以美伊地緣政治風險近來有升溫的現象，油價上彈再
度引發通膨升溫的擔憂，市場仍持續定價聯準會九月升息的可能性，美元指數有望
獲得支撐。中國國內方面，人行中間價格接連兩日創近三年新高，釋放人民幣偏強
信號， 支撐人民幣延續升值的方向 。 技術面方面，USD/CNH 匯價居於各天期均線下
方，且均線向下發散，加以匯價有效跌破 6.7150 關鍵支撐位，空方動能較盛，顯示
人民幣將延續升值的態勢。綜合以上，預估本週人民幣走勢偏升。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6800-6.7300

### Page 5

5  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/

美元兌日圓匯率走勢  
         Sep/7/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週 USD/JPY 先上後下。週初，因美伊不確定性再次升溫，油價上漲推升通膨
預期，美元自近期的低點反彈，帶領 USD/JPY 上升到 160 上方；週間，市場對日本
官方可能採取支撐匯率措施的預期升溫，且傳出日本央行可能在近期會議上宣布升
息及加快正常化步伐，帶動日圓連續兩天大幅走升；臨近週末，美元指數止穩於年
線附近，USD/JPY 跌深反彈，終場收在 156.24，週線大跌 381 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 USD/JPY 區間偏空震盪，主要原因如下：一、日圓上週飆升，數據證
實非官方干預，主因日銀審議委員高田創釋出鷹派信號，市場押注 9 月升息機率達
75%，疊加聯準會理事沃勒釋放按兵不動傾向 ，美日利差有望縮窄推升日圓。二 、美
國 8 月非農新增就業達 16.2 萬人遠超預期，帶動9 月升息機率上升至 62%，並強化
年底前升息兩次之押注。儘管川普發出中斷貿易威脅強推降息，但在就業穩健與主
席華許偏鷹派立場下，決策焦點全面轉向本週即將公布 CPI 數據，美日利差及油價
壓力預計將限縮 USD/JPY 下行空間。三、就技術面而言，日線圖中，USD/JPY 在日
央加速升息的預期下急跌，然匯價於 155 整數關卡止跌，若能有效跌破則可能還有
一波較大的跌幅。綜合以上，預估USD/JPY 本週呈現區間偏空震盪的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌日幣：154.00-158.00 
前週交易區間 
155.27 160.39

6  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/

美元兌歐元匯率走勢  
         Sep/7/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
    上週 EUR/USD 震盪走升。週初，歐元跌深反彈，不過在美伊不確定性再次升
溫及美國利率維持偏高路徑的定價支撐美元需求下，EUR/USD 弱勢整理；週間，市
場在等待美國就業相關數據公布，歐元持續走落，最低來到 1.1565；臨近週末，美
國就業數據好於預期，但聯準會官員鴿派言論，加上川普發言使升息預期受抑制，
帶動歐元先下後上，終場收在 1.1613，週線上漲 33 點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、美國 8 月非農就業增長超
預期，帶動交易員對聯準會本月重啟升息的押注顯著升溫。對此，美國總統川普上
週五強烈抨擊高利率使美國陷入劣勢，揚言若聯準會不調降借貸成本，美國將直接
停止與對美享有貿易順差的國家進行貿易 ， 意圖以極端貿易禁令迫使央行寬鬆。二、
受能源推動，歐元區 8 月通膨升至 3.3%，但核心通膨降至 2.4%，顯示油價上漲未
出現進一步滲透至工資、終端服務定價等長期通膨預期的二輪效應。歐洲央行管委
科赫示警通膨上行風險，強化 9 月升息預期，短線上為歐元提供支撐。三、技術面
而言 ， 在日線圖中 ，EUR/USD 匯價在多次向下測試 1.1550 的關鍵價位均獲得支撐 ，
目前承壓於年線但獲得月線支撐。綜合以上，預估 EUR/USD 本週呈現區間震盪的
可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 歐元兌美元：1.1540-1.1680 
前週交易區間 
1.1565 1.1641

7  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
                         新台幣利率走勢分析

115.08.31-115.09.04 
銀行隔夜拆款利率 0.805% ~ 0.830%  
30 天期短票利率   1.66% ~ 1.68% 
公債－10 年期-A15106   1.90% 
－ 5 年期- A15108   1.82%

一、 台幣貨幣市場部分，本週邁入新的提存期，因應 9 月有升息預期及季節性用
款因素 ， 銀行間調度偏保守 ，惟整體市場資金供需仍屬平穩， 令短率持穩於近期
區間。 
週四央行標售 364 天期定存單 1200 億元 ， 加權平均得標利率為1.643%， 符合
市場預期區間 1.64%-1.66%，較上期標售結果上升 1.7 個基本點。 
週 五 隔 夜 拆 款 利 率 區 間 在 0.805%~0.830% ， 30 天 期 短 票 主 要 成 交 在
1.66%~1.68%，利率大致持平於上週。 
二、 本週市場交投清淡，5 年期指標券 A15108 週二搓合成交在 1.82%，10 年期指
標券 A15106 則未成交，最近成交利率為 1.90%。 
週二標售 5 年期公債得標利率 1.82%， 落在市場預期區間1.80%~1.82%上緣 ，
投標倍數 1.57 倍為 2024 年 11 月以來最低 ， 顯示在國際債市氣氛偏空影響下 ，
市場投標態度謹慎。 
韓國、日本央行處於升息循環階段、聯準會主席 Warsh 暗示若 PCE 未往 2%
目標邁進將有升息可能 ， 在台灣經濟成長與通膨維持高位下 ， 台灣央行面臨緊
縮壓力 ， 加上美伊戰事再度升溫 ， 美債殖利率將呈震盪偏升格局 ， 預期台債殖
利率將隨美債走勢維持高檔盤整，預估 5 年券區間為 1.80%~1.85%，10 年券
區間為 1.90%至 1.95%。

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
The likelihood of an additional rate hike has risen enough
to make a November hike (+25bp to 4.6%) the base case
again. When we last changed our view on RBA policy, we
emphasised that risks of further hikes should not be ruled
out. We also said that it was prudent to continue pricing in

some chance of a November hike. But with the data having
consistently broken to the soft side in the lead-up to the
August meeting, and pass-through from fuel to non-fuel
prices easing, that probability dropped below 50% for
a time. The data flow and narrative have since changed
direction and shifted that probability higher again.
The main reasons for the shift are the growing evidence of a
more resilient household sector, and a larger-than-expected

impetus from the spillovers from the data centre boom.
National accounts and internal data have pointed to stronger
household incomes in Q2 and onwards, which means spending
will be more resilient over the near term even with sentiment
stuck at historically weak levels. Although the housing market
is looking weaker than our previously published forecasts, its
impact on consumer spending is more than offset by the wider
boost coming from a globally-driven surge in tech-related

spending. An unprecedented pipeline of investment in data
centres and associated renewable electricity generation and
distribution is expected to drive business investment and so
GDP growth – but also limit the pace of disinflation.
Tactically, we believe RBA leadership would strongly favour
a November hike over September. Overreacting to a noisy
monthly print is something they have previously said they would
not do. That said, if the internal members felt the situation was

more urgent and wanted to get the hike done in September, we
believe they could muster a majority of Monetary Policy Board
(MPB) votes in favour. Either way, the September decision may
see a split vote, with some members coming into the meeting
with different views about supply capacity and the state of the
labour market. On balance, we do not think a September hike
this is the most likely outcome and expect the MPB to prefer to
wait for the full quarterly inflation data and revised forecasts to

confirm the need for a rate increase. Clearly the probability of
the September scenario is not zero, however. We will therefore
be watching today’s RBA communication closely for signs that
the leadership is anxious to move.
Domestic demand growth forecasts have been upgraded, and
(to a lesser extent) so are our GDP forecasts. Data centre and
renewables investment are highly import-intensive. In addition,
much of the upside surprise on household spending has been

showing up in (imported) EVs and overseas travel. Much of the
upgrade to domestic demand growth is therefore matched by
Forecast update: firmer growth, higher cash rate
Forecast changes
• A full update to our forecasts has been published in our
September Market Outlook .
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
higher imports, with a smaller net impact on GDP growth. There
are enough spillovers to domestic activity, though, for us to
upgrade the expected trough in GDP growth to around 1.5%yr

over 2026, compared with the 1.0%yr we had anticipated prior
to the June quarter National Accounts. This is still below the
RBA’s downbeat view of trend growth – and further below our
own – implying spare capacity will emerge over time.
More broadly, we have been revisiting the forecasts and
overarching narratives behind those forecasts ahead of our
September Market Outlook, to be published on Friday. Growth
is stronger and housing prices are weaker. The tech sector is

adding new cost pressures not evident previously. But while
Q3 inflation is looking to be stronger than Q2 (and Q4), the
higher cash rate profile means that the inflation outlook for
2027 and 2028 is little changed from before.  The underlying
story is one of a global technology and investment boom
driving stronger business investment in Australia, supported
by healthy business balance sheets. The handover from strong
public demand growth has happened, and supply capacity is

rising, as infrastructure projects complete and in line with the
shorter lead times on data centre production.
We have not changed our assessment of the timing or
number of cuts to the cash rate, pencilling in three 25bp cuts
starting August 2027. This leaves the whole path for the cash
rate 25bps higher than our earlier expectations, consistent
with the emerging impetus from business investment. We
do not have strong conviction about the exact timing of the

unwind of restrictive monetary policy, but we expect the RBA
to take a cautious approach given last year’s experience and
the Bank’s downbeat starting assessments of capacity, labour
supply and productivity growth. Trimmed mean inflation ends
2028 somewhat below the midpoint of the RBA’s 2–3% target
range, and there are scenarios where inflation undershoots the
target entirely in late 2028 or 2029.
The table below shows the high-level forecasts motivating

our change in rates view. Further detail has been published in
Market Outlook today.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: frayed nerves unsettle expectations
continued to edge up in Q2, maintaining full employment and
indicating marginal labour supply is available to meet demand.
The US PPI subsequently met expectations in August, growth

accelerating from a revised 0.1% (prev 0.0%) in July to 0.4%.
Excluding food and energy, growth was a touch weaker than
expected at 0.2%, offset by a 0.1ppt upward revision to July
to 0.3%. Annual PPI inflation accelerated to 5.4%, or 4.6%
excluding food and energy.
Coming after last Friday’s above expectations August nonfarm
payroll release, in which 162k new positions were reported
along with 55k in back revisions to June and July, and given the

latest surge in the price of oil, the August PPI result further
solidified the market’s concerns over inflation risks, the US
10-year ranging up to just below 5.0% as a result. At the short
end, participants are now debating whether to price in a fourth
hike by the FOMC over the coming year, with three 25bp
increases fully priced by April.
As expected, and unsurprisingly given the above data and
market developments, the ECB then delivered a 25bp hike

at their September meeting. The risks were characterised as
skewed towards prices, with inflation "to remain well above
target for an extended period" primarily as a result of the
effect on energy prices of the Middle East conflict and the
Russia / Ukraine war.
The "new ECB staff projections sees headline inflation
averaging 3.0% in 2026, 2.5% in 2027 and 2.1% in 2028".
"For inflation excluding energy and food, the baseline foresees
2.5% in 2026, 2.6% in 2027 and 2.3% in 2028." These

forecasts are unchanged for 2026, but higher in 2027 and
2028 than in June. Despite the persistence in inflation and
need for tighter policy, the ECB remains constructive on the
growth outlook. "The baseline projection for economic growth
is 0.9% for 2026, 1.4% for 2027 and 1.5% for 2028. This is an
upward revision for both 2026 and 2027, mainly reflecting the
greater than expected resilience of the euro area economy."
The Governing Council is acutely aware of the uncertainty and

risks ahead, and are committed to determining policy meeting
by meeting. From their guidance, it is clear the Council are
comfortable with their current position and capacity to adjust
policy in real time to meet their medium-term mandate.
After reviewing last week’s National Accounts, we revised
our RBA call, reinstating a 25bp November rate hike.
Domestic demand is proving more resilient than we had
anticipated, buoyed by stronger household incomes and

a large pipeline of data centre and renewable energy
investment, limiting the pace of disinflation.
Markets are pricing in a circa 80% chance of a September
rate hike, but we believe the Board will prefer to wait for
confirmation from the full quarterly inflation data and its
revised forecasts. Our own forecast updates will be published
in our September Market Outlook later today on Westpac IQ.
This week’s sentiment surveys also served as a reminder that

downside risks to growth are still present in Australia.
Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment  buckled in September, the
5.2% decline to 84.4 unwinding much of August’s gain and
leaving sentiment near the pessimistic lows of early-2026.
Cost-of-living pressures clearly remain front of mind,
highlighted by the 9.2% drop in ‘family finances vs a year ago’.
The recent rebound in fuel prices, renewed concerns about
inflation's persistence and anxiety over the need for additional

monetary tightening all weighed heavily in the month. These
concerns, alongside the downbeat assessment on housing
markets, also produced a more pessimistic reading on broader
economic conditions, with both the one-year and five-year
ahead measures down 4.0% and 4.5% in the month.
Businesses are hardly brimming with optimism either, the
August NAB business survey reporting that business confidence
slipped further into negative territory to –8 in August. Conditions

also fell 5pts to –1, the weakest reading since the pandemic.
Weaker profitability was the chief culprit behind the decline,
slumping to its lowest level in years as firms grapple with
softer demand, higher interest rates and elevated input costs.
Indeed, gauges of purchase costs and labour cost growth
continue to run at above-average levels, and trading conditions
have weakened to cycle lows. As for the consumer, downside
risks persist for business.

Offshore, the flow of data was light ahead of last night’s ECB
meeting and tonight’s US CPI update.
Data for the Euro Area was constructive early in the week,
GDP growth revised up from 0.4% to 0.6% in the third
estimate for Q2, taking the annual rate up to 1.2%yr. In the
quarter, household consumption showed strength, up 0.4%,
and government expenditure lifted 0.2%. Business investment
was a partial offset, edging down 0.1%. Employment also
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics

Ryan Wells, Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Economy shows resilience, but new risks looming
The most recent data continues to show that the New
Zealand economy has held its ground, better than we dared
to hope in the early stages of the Middle East oil shock.
We’re expecting a modest rise in next Thursday’s June

quarter GDP figures, but seasonal distortions mean that
this will likely understate the true pace of growth. We’re
far from out of the woods though, with looming risks from
a resurgence in oil prices, the impending election, and the
prospect of an El Niño summer.
We’re now expecting a 0.2% rise in GDP for the June quarter .
That’s an upgrade from our previous forecast of a 0.1% decline,
reflecting the final sectoral indicators that were released
over the last week. This would see the annual growth rate

accelerate from 1.5% to 2.4%, due to an unusually weak June
quarter last year (-0.7%) dropping out of the calculation.
As we’ve noted in the past , the calculation of GDP leads to
seasonal distortions, which currently have a negative impact
on June quarter growth rates. We estimate that this will
subtract around 0.3ppts from growth this time, implying a
healthier-looking underlying rate of 0.5% for the quarter. This
would follow a 0.8% rise in the March quarter that was itself

probably overstated to the tune of 0.3ppts. In other words, the
true picture is probably one of sustained modest growth rather
than a slowdown.
Our forecast is higher than the flat outturn that the RBNZ
expected in its September Monetary Policy Statement (which
didn’t have the benefit of the most recent sectoral data). A
stronger than expected GDP result could certainly assuage
some of the committee members’ concerns about downside
risks to growth, but on balance we suspect they will be looking

more to the evidence on inflation (such as the NZIER business
survey on the 6th of October and the CPI on the 22nd), and
emerging evidence of economic momentum through the
September quarter and beyond, to determine their next move.
Since the Monetary Policy Statement , committee members
have been giving several media interviews and reproducing
their comments on the RBNZ website – part of a welcome push
this year towards greater transparency. This week featured

interviews by Prasanna Gai, who has been a hawkish-leaning
and seemingly quite influential member since the Middle East
conflict began. On this occasion, Gai’s views came across as a
little more balanced, which contributed to the outperformance
of New Zealand interest rates early in the week.
While Gai acknowledged that he had voted for a pre-emptive
OCR increase in May, he stressed that this view had always
been conditional on inflation expectations and firms’ pricing

behaviour. Given that these indicators have remained relatively
contained, he said he now viewed the decision to leave the OCR
unchanged in May as appropriate. Perhaps more surprisingly,
Gai said he did not hold a strong view on whether the OCR
would ultimately need to move into restrictive territory.
Gai also provided insight into his thinking on the neutral OCR.
He argued that the neutral rate is inherently uncertain and
should be viewed as a range rather than a precise estimate.

While geopolitical tensions, infrastructure rebuilding, defence
spending and other global investment demands could place
upward pressure on the neutral interest rate, he stressed that
the magnitude of any such shift is highly uncertain. For now, he
said he was comfortable with the RBNZ’s current estimate of
neutral and added that it was plausible that the OCR is already
operating within the neutral zone.
Overall, the interview suggested Gai remains alert to inflation

risks but that he is not advocating an aggressive tightening
path, instead placing significant weight on incoming evidence
and the evolving balance between inflation and growth risks.
On that score, rather than focusing on near-term inflation
outcomes, Gai emphasised the importance of forward-looking
indicators such as inflation expectations and survey evidence
on firms’ pricing intentions.
Cruel summer
A strong El Niño pattern has been developing in the Pacific

Ocean and is tracking to be one of the largest on record –
we’ve seen references to a “Godzilla” event as far back as May.
El Niño typically means a high risk of hot, dry conditions across
our eastern regions and the upper North Island, which includes
some of our key farming regions.
We’ve released a report this week  looking at the potential
impacts of a drought this summer: where the effects may
fall, the potential mitigants, and the macro implications. Past

severe droughts have subtracted as much as one percentage
point from GDP growth, though we’d emphasise that this
represents an upper bound rather than a forecast this time
around. Every event plays out differently, and the farming
sector has evolved towards more resilience in the almost
30 years since the last El Niño-led drought. Nevertheless,
this gives us an idea of how far we might need to adjust our
forecasts for 2027 as things develop.
Michael Gordon

Senior Economist

### Page 5

NZ: Aug REINZ House Sales and Prices (%yr) 
Sep 15, Sales Last: –10.0, Prices Last: –0.4
The housing market remained soft in July, with sales falling to 
their lowest level in two years and prices remaining broadly flat. 
Conditions remain stronger in the south, with prices up on last 
year in Canterbury, Otago and Southland, while Auckland and 
Wellington remain the weakest markets. Fixed-term mortgage 
rates rose further in August in anticipation of RBNZ tightening, 
and a growing supply of unsold homes on the market means 
that buyers are under no pressure to bid prices higher.
NZ: Aug Retail Card Spending (%mth)
Sep 15, Last: 1.3, Westpac f/c: –0.2
Retail spending rose 1.3% in July. That was in part due to 
large increases in discretionary spending categories, including 
household durables (e.g. household furnishings). Spending 
in such areas can be lumpy on a month-to-month basis, and 
a reversal is likely this month. As a result, we’re forecasting 
a small fall in spending in August. Adding to the downward 
pressures has been the continued high level of fuel prices, which 
pushed higher over the month. Retail spending has been very 
volatile in the wake of the fuel price shock. Stepping back and 
looking at the longer-term trend, spending growth has been 
relatively modest in recent months.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
NZ: Q3 Westpac-McDermott Miller Consumer
Confidence (index)
Sep 16, Last: 80.4
Our latest consumer confidence survey was in the field in
the first two weeks of September.  In the previous survey,
consumer confidence fell sharply as the impacts from the war

in the Middle East rippled across the global economy through
to wallets here in New Zealand. Since that time, fuel prices
have dropped back, but they remain high. We’ve also had the
RBNZ starting to lift the Official Cash Rate. At the same time,
we seen continued strength in commodity export prices, which
is helping to boost incomes in many regions.
NZ: Q2 Current Account Balance (% of GDP)
Sep 16, Last: –3.6, Westpac f/c: –3.7, Market f/c: –3.9
As usual, the release of the June quarter Balance of Payments

will see Stats NZ incorporate revisions based on annual
benchmarks. Revisions to goods and services trade data, already
released earlier this month, point to a slightly smaller current
account deficit in the year to March. However, this impact
could be offset or amplified by yet to be published revisions to
investment income flows. On the assumption that the latter
revisions are small, we expect the annual current account deficit
will widen in the June quarter to 3.7% of GDP, with higher

energy prices contributing to strong growth in imports.
NZ: Q2 GDP (%qtr)
Sep 17, Last: 0.8, Westpac f/c: 0.2, Market f/c: 0.1
We expect a 0.2% rise in GDP for the June quarter. That’s an
improvement on our earlier forecasts of a small decline, with
the New Zealand economy weathering the Middle East oil
shock better than we hoped. Retail spending, and particularly
hospitality, was the most visible casualty of the conflict, as
higher fuel prices squeezed household budgets. But other

sectors such as construction, wholesaling and agriculture
continued to benefit from the growth-supportive factors
that were already in place: the low level of interest rates and
strong demand for our key exports.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
NZ: Aug Selected Consumer Prices (%mth)
Sep 18
Stats NZ’s monthly prices update is expected to leave us with
a picture of annual headline inflation tracking in the high-3’s
through the second half of the year, with core inflation at
firm levels. Looking at some of the big groups, we expect a

2% rise in petrol prices and a 0.4% rise in food prices. On the
downside, further softness in housing rents is likely. A key area
to watch will be the volatile travel categories. Last month saw
large increases in the cost of services like airfares. While those
increases are likely seasonal, given elevated fuel costs those
prices could remain high for some time. That would reinforce a
firm picture for underlying inflation.
US: FOMC Policy Decision (%)

Sep 16-17, Last: 3.625, Westpac f/c: 3.875
Market f/c: 3.625, Range: 3.625 to 3.875
Recent developments in the US and Middle East require
an urgent reset of expectations across markets. Fears over
inflation need to be quelled, both for the short and long-term.
And the Committee needs to be seen to react to the market’s
concerns and give clear guidance on their approach into 2027.
As such, while we believe the underlying inflation detail is
supportive of the return of headline inflation to 2.0%yr in the

medium-term, we expect the FOMC to raise the fed funds rate
by 25bps in September and to follow up with another 25bp
hike in Q4. On hold policy through 2027 is then expected to
allow disinflation to progress, justifying two cuts in 2028.
Downside risks remain for activity, but uncertainty over
inflation is the dominant concern.

Anthropic 为旗下编程助⼿ Claude Code 推出 Projects 重⼤升级，引⼊多线程⾃动化⼯作流
架构，允许 AI 在⽤⼾离线状态下持续并⾏执⾏编程任务，标志着 AI 编程⼯具向⾃主项⽬管
理迈出实质性⼀步。该功能⽬前以测试版向部分 Claude Pro 和 Max 订阅⽤⼾开放。
摘要：
9 ⽉ 17 ⽇， Anthropic 旗下编程助⼿ Claude Code 正式推出 Projects 功能重⼤升级，将 AI 辅助开发
从单次对话模式升级为多线程⾃动化⼯作流，标志着 AI 编程⼯具向⾃主项⽬管理迈出实质性⼀
步。
本次更新的核⼼增量在于执⾏⽅式：⽤⼾不再需要逐个开启对话、⼿动跟踪每个⼦任务，只需
描述 " 需要做什么 " ， Claude 就会拆解任务、分配线程、并⾏推进。官⽅公告称，这些线程在⽤
⼾关闭笔记本电脑后仍会继续⼯作。
Anthropic 表⽰，该功能⽬前以测试版向部分 Claude Pro 和 Max 订阅⽤⼾开放。未来数周内将
逐步扩⼤访问权限，并最终覆盖 Team 和 Enterprise 计划⽤⼾。

对于开发者⽽⾔，这⼀升级直接改变了多任务项⽬的协作⽅式。过去需要⼿动切换会话、拼接
输出结果的⼯作流程，现在可以交由 Claude 统⼀调度，潜在影响涵盖从个⼈开发者到企业⼯程
团队的各类⽤⼾群体。
线程架构： Claude 扮演 " 参谋⻓ " ⻆⾊
新版 Projects 的核⼼设计是 " 协调者 + ⼯作线程 " 双层架构。
⽤⼾在项⽬主聊天界⾯发出⾼层指令， Claude 充当参谋⻓⻆⾊，将任务路由⾄新建或已有的⼯
作线程；每条线程则是⼀个独⽴的 Claude Code 云端会话，在各⾃的代码分⽀和仓库副本上执⾏
具体⼯作。

### Page 2

以实际场景为例：⽤⼾可以设定⽬标为降低应⽤结账环节的 P75 延迟， Claude 随即会分配多个
并⾏线程分别对各接⼝进⾏性能分析、测试优化⽅案并提交 Pull Request 。
⼜或者同时连接 API 、 Web 和移动端三个代码仓库，由 Claude 为每个仓库创建独⽴线程，完成废
弃接⼝的迁移、测试和 PR 提交，并告知⽤⼾哪些 PR 需要优先合并。
若不同线程修改了同⼀段代码，系统将以标准合并冲突的⽅式处理，与常规 PR 流程保持⼀致。
此外，单条线程还可进⼀步调⽤⼦代理、循环和⼯作流来拆分⼤型任务，以加快完成速度。
共享记忆：上下⽂跨会话持续积累
新版 Projects 专为⻓周期或代理式⼯作流设计，⽀持跨时间、跨会话的上下⽂积累。
所有线程共享同⼀记忆池， Claude 会随时间推移不断沉淀项⽬细节，例如记住发布⽇期调整为
周五、某功能被废弃的原因，或在修改账单服务前需要知会哪位同事，从⽽减少⽤⼾反复编写
提⽰词的负担。
在个性化层⾯， Claude 还会记住⽤⼾的⼯作⻛格和沟通偏好。⽤⼾可以要求它调整进度汇报频

率、新建线程的节奏以及更新内容的详细程度。
与记忆功能并⾏， Projects 还新增了 "Library" （素材库），⾃动归集⽤⼾上传的⽂件以及
Claude ⽣成的各类产出物，⽅便后续⼯作调取历史材料、在已有成果基础上继续推进。
使⽤限制与后续计划
由于⼀个项⽬可同时运⾏多条线程，且每条线程均为完整的 Claude Code 云端会话，Projects 的
⽤量消耗速度较单⼀会话模式明显加快。
Anthropic 提⽰⽤⼾注意项⽬级别的⽤量监控，并⽀持分别为协调者聊天和⼯作线程选择模型及
算⼒等级，以便灵活管控成本。
在功能路线图⽅⾯， Anthropic 表⽰线程⽬前仅在云端运⾏，⽀持在本地机器上运⾏、调⽤本地
⼯具和代码、以及接⼊内⽹的功能即将推出。
就当前的测试版访问资格⽽⾔，仅限在 Claude Code 中使⽤云端会话、且在⽹⻚端或桌⾯端尚
⽆已有 Projects 的 Pro 和 Max ⽤⼾。

Document 261: Figure租了30套房，让机器人挨家“考核家务”

Source PDF: all report pdf/Figure租了30套房，让机器人挨家“考核家务”.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-09-18
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

Figure 发布 Helix 2.5 ，验证⼈形机器⼈零样本全⾝⾃主能⼒：在 30 套陌⽣住宅中，不采数
据、不额外训练，零样本成功率从 9% 提升⾄ 56% 。模型基于 Index ⼈类⾏为数据预训练，
实现⾏为泛化，并⾸次测量 “ ⼈类到机器⼈迁移缩放定律 ” 。
摘要：
当地时间 9 ⽉ 17 ⽇，⼈形机器⼈公司 Figure 发布神经⽹络模型 Helix 2.5 ，并公布了⼀组零样本全
⾝⾃主实验结果：在没有任何新数据采集与环境适配的情况下，机器⼈的零样本成功率从 9% 暴
增⾄ 56% 。
Figure 创始⼈兼 CEO 布雷特 · 阿德科克（ Brett Adcock ）表⽰，公司租下旧⾦⼭湾区的 30 套住
宅，让 Helix 2.5 在不采集新数据、不额外训练、也不做环境适配的前提下直接执⾏任务。 Figure
要回答的是：机器⼈能否进⼊从未⻅过的家庭，直接开始做家务？
官⽅博客将 Helix 2.5 称为 Figure 迄今最先进的神经⽹络，旨在验证⼈形机器⼈能否仅凭已有知

识，在未经微调的环境中⾃主完成⻓时程任务。
⼈类能⾛进陌⽣房屋并⽴即投⼊⼯作，是因为对物理世界的认知具备跨环境迁移能⼒，⽆需因
床⾼或家具布局变化重新学习。传统机器⼈通常依赖在具体环境中采集数据并做针对性训练。
Figure 希望打破这⼀范式：先让机器⼈从⼤规模⼈类⾏为经验中学习，再将其通⽤能⼒直接迁
移到陌⽣真实环境。
租 30 套房，不采数据，直接⼲活
在官⽅演⽰中， Figure 03 ⼈形机器⼈进⼊⼀套完全陌⽣的房⼦，连续完成三项⻓时程全⾝任
务：整理客厅、铺床、折⽑⼱。
Helix 2.5 在真实家庭中完成整理客厅、狭窄空间穿⾏铺床与折⽑⼱三项全⾝移动操纵任务
整理客厅时，散落各处的玩具被机器⼈逐⼀捡起放⼊篮⼦。阿德科克在相关视频和帖⼦中强
调，机器⼈此前从未进⼊这些房间，公司没有为这些新环境采集数据。机器⼈需要⼀边移动、
⼀边寻找⽬标物体、调整⾝体姿态，再完成抓取和放置。
铺床任务更难。机器⼈从未进过这个房间，从未⻅过这张床，也从未⻅过这些床上⽤品。它必
须抓起枕头、拉动被⼦，把相关物体移动到指定位置，并将被⼦拉平。折⽑⼱则被 Figure ⽤来测

试对可变形物体的操作能⼒：机器⼈需要抓起此前没有⻅过的⽑⼱，完成折叠，再将其放⼊篮
⼦。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

这三项任务在不同陌⽣家庭中结合了主动感知、移动与操纵
Figure 表⽰， Helix 2.5 是公司构建过的最先进神经⽹络。⼀个经过 Index 预训练的基础模型被⽤
于三个不同任务，覆盖移动、刚性与可变形物体操作、双⼿协调以及主动感知。
这些任务之所以被选中，是因为它们要求同时解决多个经典机器⼈问题：感知、移动、操纵、
双⼿协调以及全⾝控制，⽽且必须实时完成。⼤部分机器⼈泛化研究都建⽴在围绕机器建造的
环境中，例如桌⾯机械臂局限于固定⼯作空间，轮式机器⼈则需要⾜够开阔的地⾯供底盘移动
和转向。但家庭环境不会配合机器⼈，⼈形机器⼈必须让⾃⼰的⾝体成为任务的⼀部分，在狭
窄、杂乱的空间中移动、观察、伸⼿和操纵物体。
这⾥还有⼀个需要特别说明的概念： Figure 所说的 “ 零样本 ” ，主要针对评估环境和被操作物体。
30 套评估住宅此前没有采集数据，评估使⽤的玩具、⽑⼱和床上⽤品也没有出现在任务指定数
据中。机器⼈直接使⽤各个陌⽣家庭中的沙发、床和折叠⽑⼱完成任务。
换句话说， Helix 2.5 并不是完全没有接受过 “ 整理客厅、铺床、折⽑⼱ ” 的任务训练。三个⾏为

本⾝经过了在其他环境采集的任务指定数据适配， Figure 真正想验证的是：这些已经学会的⾏
为，能否在从未⻅过的住宅和物体上直接泛化，⽽不需要到了现场再重新训练。
评估标准极其苛刻：整理客厅要求把 13 ⾄ 15 个玩具全部收好，折⽑⼱要求完整折叠⼊篮，铺床
要求枕头与被⻆拉⾄床顶三分之⼀并平整。任务要求端到端完全成功，不提供任何部分得分；
每个任务均使⽤同⼀个固定模型节点（测试版本），不针对新环境调整模型参数；过程只要出现
⼀次⼈类安全⼲预，便直接判定失败。
为零样本评估将场景重置为⽆控制初始条件的⽰例
Figure 表⽰，它将 Helix 2.5 部署到 30 套此前没有进⼊过的湾区住宅。每次试验开始前，评估物体
都会被提前放置，并由 AI 模型结合⼈⼯审核确认这些物体没有出现在任务指定数据中。每⼀次试
验还使⽤独⽴的初始配置，并对所有被评估策略采⽤相同的重置条件，以保证⽐较公平。
在试验层⾯，官⽅博客给出的关键数据是：零样本成功率从 9% 提升到 56% ，即提升⾄原来的 6
倍以上。这⾥的 “ 成功 ” 指完整任务成功，⽽不是完成其中⼀部分。

Index 预训练，⼈类经验是机器⼈的 “ 漏洞 ”

### Page 3

Helix 2.5 的关键不只是模型，⽽是它背后的 Index 。
Figure 的逻辑很简单：⼤语⾔模型之所以能够不断扩⼤能⼒，⼀个重要原因是互联⽹提供了海
量⼈类数据，使预训练能够随着数据和算⼒规模扩⼤⽽持续获得收益。但机器⼈没有互联⽹规
模的⾏为数据。 Figure 因此试图建⽴⼀个⼤规模的⼈类⾏为数据集 Index ，让机器⼈先从⼈类如
何在真实世界中⾏动、抓取和操纵物体的经验中学习。
Figure 在此前关于 Index 的介绍中，将其定位为全球规模的⼈类⾏为数据集。 Helix 2.5 则进⼀步
验证：这些⼈类经验能否转化成真正的机器⼈⾏为能⼒。
Figure 表⽰，⽬前 Index 每秒能够产⽣约 35 分钟的新的⼈类经验数据。与此同时，公司已经承诺
投⼊ 35 亿美元算⼒训练 Helix 。
最关键的问题是：这些⼈类经验，真的能转化为机器⼈⾏为吗？
Figure 做了⼀组控制实验：两个模型使⽤完全相同的任务指定数据，架构、优化⽅式、超参数、
下游数据和评估⽅法全部保持⼀致，唯⼀变量就是是否进⾏了  Index 预训练。

实验对⽐了两个模型：⼀个是从零开始随机初始化权重的基线模型，另⼀个则是先经过 Index 海
量数据预训练的 Helix 2.5 模型。 Helix 2.5 本⾝完全是从随机权重开始、仅依靠 Index 数据完成预
训练的，这与上⼀代 Helix 02 截然不同。 Helix 02 当初是基于现成的预训练视觉语⾔模型进⾏初
始化的。
30 套家庭中的零样本成功率对⽐（从头训练模型 vs 基于 Index 预训练模型）
结果⾮常明显。没有 Index 预训练的策略，零样本成功率只有 9% ；加⼊ Index 预训练后，成功率
达到 56% ，超过前者 6 倍。由于预训练是唯⼀的实验变量， Figure 认为，这⼀差距可以直接衡量
Index 预训练对零样本能⼒的贡献。
⽽且， Index 的训练数据并不是专⻔围绕这三个测试任务构建的。 Figure 表⽰，没有任何⼀个评
估任务占 Index 预训练数据的⽐例超过 1.90% 。这意味着模型获得的预训练经验并不是简单地 “ 反
复学习如何折⽑⼱、铺床和收玩具 ” ，⽽是试图从更加⼴泛的⼈类⾏为中形成可迁移的能⼒。

### Page 4

Helix 2.5 与⼀个执⾏相同任务的 Helix 02 进⾏⽐较
数据效率同样发⽣变化。 Figure 将 Helix 2.5 与⼀个执⾏相同任务的 Helix 02 策略进⾏⽐较：后者
使⽤在实际评估环境中直接采集的数据，⽽ Helix 2.5 在达到相当成功率的情况下，只需要⼀半
的适应数据，并进⼀步将⾏为泛化到 30 套此前没有⻅过的住宅。
Figure 因此将这⼀结果概括为：⾏为指定成本降低 2 倍，同时部署范围扩⼤ 30 倍。
另⼀个值得关注的变化，是 Helix 2.5 的全⾝⾃我纠错能⼒。在⻓时程任务中，机器⼈并⾮每⼀步
都能⼀次完成。如果抓取或者折叠出现偏差，它可能后退重新定位、改变站姿，甚⾄绕到整张
床的另⼀侧继续完成任务。 Figure 将这种 “ 犯错后恢复并继续推进 ” 的能⼒，视为 Index 预训练带
来的重要效果之⼀。
更⻓远看， Figure 还报告了⼀条 “ ⼈类到⼈形机器⼈ ” 的迁移缩放定律。团队在 Index 的嵌套⼦集
上训练四个模型，预训练数据规模逐步扩⼤，最⼤跨度达到  8 倍，同时保持模型规模和下游训

练不变。实验结果显⽰，随着  Index 数据量的每次翻倍，下游机器⼈对下⼀步动作预测的损失
值（ loss ），均呈现出平滑且可预测的下降趋势。
⼈类到⼈形机器⼈的迁移缩放定律图表
更为关键的是，这种下降趋势展现出极⾼的可预测性。 Figure 表⽰，仅凭较⼩数据量训练得出
的测试结果，便能在最⼤规模模型训练启动前，精准预测出其测试损失值⾄⼩数点后四位，预
测误差仅占完整 8 倍数据量变化范围的 0.54% 。

### Page 5

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
Figure 称，这是⾸次在⼈形机器⼈上测量 “ ⼈类到机器⼈迁移缩放定律 ” 。它意味着，⼀个此前主
要存在于⼤语⾔模型领域的思路，通过扩⼤预训练数据规模，预测未来模型能⼒的提升，可能
也能够延伸到⼈形机器⼈。
当然，这⾥测量的只是数据规模的缩放关系，模型规模和下游训练都保持不变。因此，它还不
能直接证明 “ 机器⼈模型越⼤就⼀定越强 ” ，但⾄少说明：在当前实验中，增加⼈类经验数据与机
器⼈动作预测能⼒之间存在可预测关系。
重新划定 “ 学习 ” 与 “ 部署 ” 的边界
从 Helix02 到 Helix 2.5 ， Figure 实际上在改变⼀个前提：机器⼈不必每进⼊⼀个新地⽅，就重新
学习⼀次。
Helix 02 曾展⽰全⾝⻓时程控制能⼒，从卸洗碗机到⾃主运⾏ 200 ⼩时物流任务，但这些系统学

习的数据来⾃机器⼈将要运⾏的地⽅。 Helix 2.5 则试图证明：全⾝智能可以从⼈类经验中学习，
并迁移到新场景，⽽不是每次进⼊⼀个新环境后重新建⽴。
这也是 Helix 2.5 与此前机器⼈系统之间真正值得关注的差别。
传统机器⼈的数据闭环往往是 “ 机器⼈进⼊环境采集数据，训练模型，再进⼊环境 ” 。这种⽅式可
以不断提⾼单⼀场景中的表现，但如果机器⼈最终要进⼊千家万⼾，就意味着每⼀个家庭都可
能成为⼀个新的数据采集和训练对象。
Figure 想建⽴的则是另⼀条路径：⼈类先在现实世界中积累经验， Index 将经验规模化， Helix
2.5 从⼈类经验中预训练，机器⼈把⾏为知识带到陌⽣环境中。
如果这⼀模式继续成⽴，那么机器⼈数据的来源就不再局限于机器⼈本⾝。⼈类已经在现实世
界中积累了数百万年的物理交互经验，⽽ Figure 想做的是把其中⼀部分转化为机器可以学习的训
练数据。
Figure 在官⽅博客中也明确强调，这并不意味着通⽤⼈形机器⼈问题已经被解决。它认为，
Helix 2.5 提供的只是第⼀个证据：全⾝智能可以从⼈类经验中学习，并转移到新的场景，⽽不需

要每次重新构建。
Figure 给出的结论⾮常直接：如果 Helix 2.5 验证了这套逻辑，那么未来投⼊更多的数据与算⼒，
就能让机器⼈在踏⼊任何新家庭之前，预先掌握更多关于物理世界的认知。
正如阿德科克在官⽅视频结尾时所说：“ 是时候扩⼤规模了（ It’s time to scale up ）。 ”当预训练
汲取⾃海量的⼈类经验，部署应⽤在陌⽣的真实家庭，具⾝智能的边界正被重新定义：机器⼈
不必每到⼀个新家就重学⼀遍，⽽是先向⼈类学习，再通往千家万⼾。
本⽂来源：腾讯科技

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The six-month annualised growth rate in the Westpac–
Melbourne Institute Leading Index, which indicates the likely
pace of economic activity relative to trend three to nine months
into the future, lifted to –0.09% in August from –0.17% in July.

The Leading Index shows momentum has continued to
improve and is only a touch below trend heading into late
2026 and early 2027. The June quarter national accounts
showed the Australian economy holding up better than
expected in the face of a global energy shock and higher
interest rates. Growth appears to be stabilising at a slow
pace rather than stalling with households resilient and
notable support coming from a strong ramp-up in data centre
investment. While a further rise in interest rates will take

some of the gloss off near term, Westpac now expects annual
growth to slow 1.5% by year-end, upgraded from our previous
forecast of 1% and broadly consistent with the improved
signal from the Leading Index.
The growth pulse is still clearly on the soft side. The Leading
Index growth rate has been below trend since the start of the
year, averaging –0.17%. This compares to the slightly above
trend reads recorded through most of 2025, which posted an

average read of +0.26%. However, the current below trend
run is noticeably milder than the weakness seen during the
protracted cost-of-living crisis that ran through most of 2022
to 2024. The average Leading Index growth read during this
period was –0.46% with lows closer to –1%.
The Leading Index growth rate is now roughly back where it
was six months ago, down only slightly from the –0.07% pace
back in February. That said, the detail points to significant

shifts in the growth mix and some fragility to the improved
signal. Component-wise the six months has seen slightly
bigger drags coming from labour markets, financial markets,
commodity prices and consumer sentiment, which have
taken 0.42ppts off the Index growth rate since February on
a combined basis. This has been balanced by a more positive
signal from dwelling approvals (adding +0.32ppts) and a
firming in US industrial production (+0.08ppts).

The mix does raise some questions about the extent to which
the recent improvement in momentum will be sustained.
Leading Index suggests momentum a touch below trend
The Westpac-Melbourne Institute Leading Index of
Economic Activity is designed to assist in identifying
turning points in the economy. The index combines
variables that reflect different aspects of the economy
into a single index that generally produces a more reliable
cyclical indicator than any single component taken

individually. The Leading Index of Economic Activity
provides advance information on the state of the economy
and gives early warnings of cyclical turning points.
Rising fuel prices and concerns about further interest rate
increases look to be weighing on consumer sentiment again
with signs that the downturn in established housing markets
is also impacting. These effects could intensify near term and
may spread beyond the consumer to other index components.

The Reserve Bank Monetary Policy Board next meets on
September 28–29. While the latest Leading Index update
still shows the growth momentum is below trend, the soft
rather than weak pace suggests the economy’s relatively
resilient performance in the June quarter is likely to extend
through the second half of the year and early part of 2027.
This performance is adding to the Board’s unease that high
inflation remains ‘sticky’ and may track a slower path back to

the 2-3% target. We expect the RBA to raise rates again but,
on balance, hold off until another comprehensive quarterly
inflation update is released on October 28. That means the
September meeting is likely to be a ‘very hawkish hold’.
Matthew Hassan
Head of Australian Macro-Forecasting

THIS WEEK IN EARNINGS 
AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS 
 26Q2 Y/Y earnings are expected to be 53.3%. Excluding the energy sector, the Y/Y earnings estimate is 49.5%. 
 Of the 492 companies in the S&P 500 that have reported earnings to date for 26Q2, 86.0% have reported earnings 
above analyst estimates. This compares to a long-term average of 67.5% and prior four quarter average of 80%. 
 26Q2 Y/Y revenue is expected to be 15.6%. Excluding the energy sector, the growth estimate is 13.5%. 
 77.3% of companies have reported 26Q2 revenue above analyst expectations. This compares to a long-term 
average of 62.8% and an average over the past four quarters of 77%. 
 For 26Q3, there have been 36 negative EPS preannouncements issued by S&P 500 corporations compared to 63 
positive EPS preannouncements. By dividing 36 by 63 the N/P ratio is 0.6 for the S&P 500 Index.  
 The forward four-quarter (26Q3– 27Q2) P/E ratio for the S&P 500 is 20.3. 
 During the week of Sep. 7, six S&P 500 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings.

There has been an increase in the share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 since the start of the quarter (to $858.9B 
from $687.1B). Two of the eleven sectors have experienced downward revisions to estimates.

Since May 1, the health care (-12.3%) and utilities (-1.5%) sectors have recorded the highest percentage decreases in 
earnings, while the communication services (100.1%) sector has recorded the highest percentage increase in 
earnings. Overall, share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 25% since the start of the quarter.

Since May 1, the health care (-9.3B) and utilities (-0.2B) sectors have recorded the highest dollar-level decreases in 
earnings, while the communication services (70B) sector has recorded the highest dollar-level increase in earnings. 
Overall, expected share-weighted earnings for the S&P 500 have increased by 171.7B since the start of the quarter.

492 companies in the S&P 500 Index have reported earnings for Q2 2026. Of these companies, 86.0% reported 
earnings above analyst expectations and 11.0% reported earnings below analyst expectations. In a typical quarter 
(since 1994), 67% of companies beat estimates and 20% miss estimates. Over the past four quarters, 80% of 
companies beat the estimates and 16% missed estimates. 
In aggregate, companies are reporting earnings that are 8.5% above estimates, which compares to a long-term (since 
1994) average surprise factor of 4.4% and the average surprise factor over the prior four quarters of 7.5%.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q2 is 53.3%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth 
rate declines to 49.5%. The S&P 500 expects to see share-weighted earnings of $858.9B in 26Q2, compared to share-
weighted earnings of $560.2B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 503 constituents) in 25Q2. 
10 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q2. The Energy and 
Communication Services sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Health Care sector 
has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q2. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (142.7%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $55.8B in 26Q2, 
compared to earnings of $23B in 25Q2. 4 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (354.1%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (166.5%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 60.1%. 
The Communication Services sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (114.2%) of any sector. It is 
expected to earn $140B in 26Q2, compared to earnings of $65.3B in 25Q2. 7 of the 9 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Cable & Satellite (397.4%) and Interactive Media & Services 
(174.3%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the 
growth rate declines to 3.5%. 
The Health Care sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (-6.5%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $65.9B in 
26Q2, compared to earnings of $70.5B in 25Q2. 2 of the 10 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Biotechnology (-67.6%) and Pharmaceuticals (-9.2%) sub-industries have the lowest 
earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 22.7%.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the S&P 500 for 26Q3 is 29.7%. If the energy sector is excluded, the growth 
rate declines to 26.3%. The S&P 500 expects to see share-weighted earnings of $768.7B in 26Q3, compared to share-
weighted earnings of $592.8B (based on the year-ago earnings of the current 503 constituents) in 25Q3. 
11 of the 11 sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in earnings relative to 25Q3. The Energy and 
Information Technology sectors have the highest earnings growth rates for the quarter, while the Consumer Staples 
sector has the weakest anticipated growth compared to 25Q3. 
The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (105%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $52.2B in 26Q3, 
compared to earnings of $25.4B in 25Q3. 4 of the 5 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see higher earnings 
than a year ago. The Oil & Gas Refining & Marketing (399%) and Integrated Oil & Gas (107.6%) sub-industries have 
the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate declines to 23.7%. 
The Information Technology sector has the second highest earnings growth rate (64.6%) of any sector. It is expected 
to earn $252.5B in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $153.5B in 25Q3. 12 of the 12 sub-industries in the sector are 
anticipated to see higher earnings than a year ago. The Semiconductors (131.1%) and Semiconductor Materials & 
Equipment (68.9%) sub-industries have the highest earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, 
the growth rate declines to 24.7%. 
The Consumer Staples sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (3.2%) of any sector. It is expected to earn $33.8B 
in 26Q3, compared to earnings of $32.8B in 25Q3. 4 of the 11 sub-industries in the sector are anticipated to see lower 
earnings than a year ago. The Brewers (-11.4%) and Packaged Foods & Meats (-5.6%) sub-industries have the lowest 
earnings growth in the sector. If these sub-industries are removed, the growth rate improves to 4.2%.

STARMINE EARNINGS SURPRISE FORECAST 
Looking forward at quarterly performance, we use StarMine’s SmartEstimate® from LSEG to determine which 
companies in the S&P 500 are better poised to beat and miss earnings estimates. The SmartEstimate® is a weighted 
average of analyst estimates, with more weight given to more recent estimates and more accurate analysts. Our 
studies have shown that when the SmartEstimate® differs significantly from the consensus (I/B/E/S mean estimate), 
the Predicted Surprise accurately predicts the direction of earnings surprises or further revisions 70% of the time. 
When a significant Predicted Surprise for revenue is also present for the period, the accuracy improves to 78%.  
StarMine ARM from LSEG is an analyst revisions stock ranking model, designed to predict future changes in analyst 
sentiment. ARM incorporates more accurate earnings estimates through the SmartEstimate prediction service. ARM 
region rankings scores companies by region on a scale of 1 to 100 where 100 represents the most bullish sentiment.  
Over the next two weeks, six S&P 500 companies are expected to report earnings. Of these companies, 0 positive 
surprises and 0 negative surprises are expected from S&P 500 companies reporting quarterly results.

Document 342: The revenge of the supply-side_ A new economic regime (2026-09-14)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The revenge of the supply-side_ A new economic regime (2026-09-14).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-09-14
Pages: 11
Status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

The revenge of the supply-side:  
A new economic regime

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Strategist

September 14, 2026 
Back in the 2010s, macroeconomic policy was preoccupied by insufficient 
demand. In historical terms, it was an unusual period of low inflation and low 
interest rates, where supply-side issues were considered peripheral. If anything, 
it went almost unquestioned that the supply side of the economy would scale up 
to meet higher demand. 
But in the 2020s so far, we’re back in a supply-driven world. Growth has been 
constrained as much by supply shortages as deficient demand. This year alone, 
we’ve seen major consequences from the closure of the Strait of Hormuz, with 
ramifications for oil prices, inflation and economic growth. 
This macro regime shift, where supply rather than demand is the bigger issue, is 
a tricky environment to navigate, and renders much of the 2010s playbook 
redundant: 
— First, inflation spikes will occur more frequently in a supply-constrained 
world, as there isn’t the spare economic capacity to meet higher 
demand. 
— Second, monetary stimulus also becomes less effective in this 
environment, as traditional demand-side monetary tools can’t deal with 
the direct cause of supply shocks. 
— Third, fiscal stimulus in a supply-constrained environment becomes 
much more inflationary and is more likely to crowd out private 
investment. 
Looking forward, the return to a supply-driven world means that the primary 
issue for policymakers will be expanding the productive frontier of the economy. 
After years where insufficient demand was the main preoccupation, the future of 
growth now rests on a supply-side expansion that can raise living standards 
across the board.

### Page 2

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
The paradigm shift in thinking: How the biggest economic
problem moved from demand to supply
The legacy of the Global Financial Crisis led to a world of demand deficiency
In 2008, the Global Financial Crisis (GFC) marked the worst economic contraction
since the Great Depression of the 1930s. Unemployment rose to multi-decade
highs and the subsequent recovery was very sluggish, despite huge economic

stimulus on a scale previously unheard of in peacetime. This slow recovery was
clear in numerous ways, but an obvious one was that the US unemployment rate
didn’t return to its pre-GFC low until 2017, a full decade later. Meanwhile in the
Eurozone, it didn’t happen until February 2020, just as the pandemic then sent it
higher once again.
This was a world where deficient demand was the main preoccupation of
macroeconomic policymaking. Indeed, the Depression-era concept of “secular

stagnation” made a comeback, in which the equilibrium interest rate consistent
with full employment was negative. In this world, the constraints of the zero lower
bound on monetary policy would make it very difficult to get to full employment.
This demand deficiency manifested itself in persistent inflation undershoots.
Throughout the 2010s, both the Federal Reserve and the ECB missed their 2%
inflation targets on the downside. Policy rates remained close to the zero lower

bound, and long-term market interest rates also moved continuously lower. Even
before the pandemic, Germany’s 10-year bund yield fell to -0.7% in summer 2019.
In this environment of negative yields, borrowing was basically a “free lunch”.
At the time, there were genuine fears about a permanent low-growth trap. Indeed,
when the Covid-19 pandemic began and another economic contraction was
underway, there were initially concerns that deflationary pressures would intensify

further. For instance, the 10-year US inflation swap (a market-based measure of
expectations for US CPI over the next decade) fell to just 0.81% in March 2020.
Investors genuinely thought that low inflation and low interest rates were likely to
persist for the decade ahead as well, and the 10-year Treasury yield closed at a
record low of 0.51% in August 2020.
This fear of deficient demand informed the policy response to Covid-19. The
stimulus packages went well beyond those seen in the GFC. For instance, no time

was lost in slashing rates back to the zero lower bound, and the fiscal deficits went
far above anything previously seen in peacetime. In fact, the US federal deficit stood
at -14.5% of GDP in 2020, the biggest since 1945. And with much of the global
economy locked down for months, consumers accumulated huge “excess
savings” as they couldn’t spend on their normal activities.
The pandemic was the turning point towards a world of supply constraints

With hindsight, we can see that all this stimulus and the accumulation of excess
savings created a huge demand surge, just as the global economy was
experiencing major supply-chain issues as it reopened:
n The lockdown periods saw a big rotation in spending away from services
(like dining out, travel, gym memberships) towards durable goods (like
home office equipment, electronics, exercise equipment). This
overwhelmed existing supply chains.
n Pandemic-related disruption led to major issues with shipping. A severe

### Page 3

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
shortage of containers led freight rates to rise more than 5-fold at their peak.
n Labour markets were disrupted, as many took early retirement during the
pandemic or changed occupations. Companies therefore had to raise
wages to compete for a smaller pool of workers.
n Commodity prices also surged in this period, particularly energy. The
pandemic period saw producers cut back on drilling and exploration. But

this meant it was more difficult to dial up supply when demand started to
rise again, and energy prices surged back, with Brent crude oil moving
above its pre-pandemic levels in mid-to-late 2021.
These supply constraints marked a major shift from the environment of the 2010s.
Back then, it was almost an unquestioned assumption that the supply-side of the
economy would scale up to meet demand. Inflation had been low and the most
obvious signs of supply-side stress were not apparent. But now the reverse was

true, and inflation was gathering pace again.
Initially, many argued that this burst of inflation would prove temporary. They
argued it was down to one-off factors like the pandemic and the subsequent
reopening. Moreover, there was a view that the longer-term disinflationary forces
of the previous 30 years would still reassert themselves. But by late-2021, it was
clear that the combination of huge stimulus, the drawdown of excess savings, and
serious supply-side disruption had created more inflation than anticipated. By

December 2021, US CPI had risen to its highest level since 1982, at 7.0%, whilst
Eurozone CPI was at its highest since 1991, at 5.0%.

The transition to a supply-constrained world accelerated dramatically in early 2022
with Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. Energy prices rose significantly, and European
industry was forced to decouple from Russian gas. Meanwhile, the blockade of
Ukraine’s Black Sea ports restricted the supply of agricultural commodities like

wheat and corn, driving food prices higher still.
This sent inflation even higher, up to multi-decade highs around the world. US CPI
inflation peaked at 9.1% in June 2022, and Eurozone CPI peaked at 10.6% in
October 2022. This was the context in which central banks launched into the most
aggressive rate hikes in a generation. Over 2022-23, the Federal Reserve hiked rates
by 525bps, and the ECB delivered 450bps.

### Page 4

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
These supply issues persisted long after the pandemic-related issues were over
Yet even as the worst of that inflation wave passed, the 2023-24 period kept
demonstrating how supply-side disruptions could lead to wider economic
problems. For instance, attacks from the Houthi rebels in the Red Sea forced major
shipping lines to reroute away from the Suez Canal around the Cape of Good Hope,

adding over a week to the length of trips. Meanwhile, an El Nino-induced drought
meant transit capacity through the Panama Canal fell 30%, forcing ships to wait in
long queues or reroute instead. And in Europe, repeated summer heatwaves have
lowered the Rhine’s water levels, disrupting industrial barges and affecting
industries like chemicals and steel.

More broadly, the other shift in the post-pandemic period was the growing use of
strategic industrial policy, particularly as governments sought to boost domestic
manufacturing capacity. For instance, the US passed the Inflation Reduction Act

under the Biden administration, which encouraged clean energy investments, as
well as the CHIPS Act, which sought to boost semiconductor manufacturing. Tariffs
also became a more enduring feature of the landscape, and the Biden
administration hiked tariffs on Chinese imports, with those on EVs up from 25% to
100%, whilst semiconductors rose from 25% to 50%.
Then under the second Trump administration, tariffs became an even bigger part of
the policy toolkit, with the US effective tariff rate surging from 2.6% beforehand to

a peak of 23% in April 2025, before settling around 11% currently. Several different
measures were introduced, but the macro impacts were clear. In particular, there
was a major wave of front-loading of imports in Q1 2025, as those trading
internationally sought to get ahead of the announcements. Indeed, the surge in
imports meant US GDP contracted slightly in Q1 2025. Moreover, other countries
moved to respond, and not just with tariffs of their own. For instance, China

introduced export controls on rare earths.
Whilst 2025 was dominated by tariffs, 2026 then brought a fresh supply shock for
the global economy with the closure of the Strait of Hormuz. Previously, around
25% of the world’s seaborne oil trade had gone through the Strait, including nearly
15 million barrels per day of crude oil. But the start of the US-Iran conflict led the
Strait of Hormuz to close, and six months later only limited traffic is still able to pass

through. This led to another supply shock, with Brent crude oil prices moving back

### Page 5

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
above $100/bbl, whilst European natural gas futures also reached their highest
levels since late-2022. Yet again, the biggest global economic disruption of the year
to date has been a supply shock, rather than a demand one.

Types of supply shocks
Broadly speaking, we can distinguish between three sorts of supply shocks,
although there can be considerable overlap between these categories.
1. Temporary supply shocks that have a short-term economic impact.
Example: low river levels blocking a key trade route.

The first category of supply shocks are those that have a temporary impact on the
economy. Examples include the blockage of the Suez Canal in 2021 when the Ever
Given container ship ran aground, or a drought that causes river levels to fall and
means fewer ships can get through key waterways.
These are supply shocks that temporarily remove capacity, but then see a bounce
back to “normal”, broadly speaking. For instance, a drought is hopefully not

repeated, and when river levels return to normal, supply can resume again.
For the economy, this means the longer-term consequences are also limited. The
inflation impact is mean-reverting, and output recovers, often in a V-shape. These
are the sort of shocks that central banks are usually able to “look through”, because
they don’t cause permanent structural damage to the economy.
2.Medium-term supply shocks that can last several years, with a clear economic

impact.
Example: the closure of the Strait of Hormuz.
These kinds of shocks are often driven by geopolitical events or policy decisions,
and are longer-lasting shocks which do not have a quick solution. A good example
is the current closure of the Strait of Hormuz, but we have seen many in recent years.
In theory, these shocks can end, but their length is uncertain and they have material
economic impacts. An increase in tariffs also fits into this category, as it forces an

adjustment in trade flows, even if they can be reversed in theory.

### Page 6

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
A good example of this kind of supply shock is the 1973 oil shock. Back in 1973, the
oil embargo by OAPEC led oil prices to nearly quadruple, and they remained at that
higher level for years after. It wasn’t simply a temporary adjustment that normalised
again.
Unlike the short-term shocks, these medium-term shocks can force a permanent
rerouting of trade flows and supply chains. Moreover, they can push inflation higher

for an extended period and are more likely to filter through into core inflation
categories. And because they are not temporary shocks that quickly adjust,
policymakers can’t “look through” them as easily. As such, central banks aren’t
able to accommodate the shock, and often have to hike rates to clamp down on the
resulting inflation, as we saw in 2022.
3.Structural supply trends that affect the longer-term growth rate.
Example: Demographic trends.

This category includes the long-term forces on the supply side that affect the
economy’s growth rate. A good example are demographics and the current
situation of ageing populations. Another example are the multi-decade processes
of globalization and deglobalization.
These are factors that can permanently shift the productive frontier of the economy.
For example, the current situation of ageing populations means that a smaller
proportion of the population are of working-age, meaning that labour forces are

shrinking. As a result, that constrains the growth rate of potential GDP, meaning
that more growth must come from higher productivity rather than a larger number
of workers.
These shifting trends can lead to permanent changes in so-called r*, which is the
equilibrium interest rate where full employment is achieved – a level that neither
stimulates nor restricts economic activity. In other words, these factors are capable
of shifting the economy’s potential growth rate, at which it can grow sustainably

without generating inflationary pressures.
What happened the last time the supply-side dominated:
Lessons from the 1970s
As we consider the return of the supply side, it’s worth bearing in mind this is far
from the first time that supply shocks have dominated the landscape. Back in the
1970s, the biggest economic shocks of that decade were also supply driven.
That decade saw two huge oil shocks in 1973 and 1979. In the first, the Organization
of Arab Petroleum Exporting Countries (OAPEC) placed an embargo on several

countries, including the United States, in retaliation for their support for Israel in the
Yom Kippur War. This caused oil prices to nearly quadruple, triggering a recession
across multiple countries that began in late 1973. Then in 1979, a second oil shock
occurred around the time of the Iranian Revolution. That coincided with a big
decline in Iran’s oil output, before the Iran-Iraq War in 1980 led to further production
declines for both countries. As a result, oil prices more than doubled again.

To be fair, there were other demand-side and supply-side shocks that meant
inflation got out of hand in the 1970s. But the two oil shocks were the single biggest
factors, and created a stagflationary environment in which inflation accelerated

### Page 7

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
and growth slowed.

Source : Finaeon, Deutsche Bank
This experience is instructive for today, as the 1970s demonstrated how a repeated
set of supply shocks could act in a cumulative way. After all, even if the textbook

response is to “look through” a temporary supply shock, a series of consecutive
shocks can suddenly feel very permanent when they happen one-after-another.
That continuous disruption can then cause inflation expectations to become
unanchored, risking the kind of dynamics that trigger wage-price spirals. Indeed,
much as economists can focus on “core inflation” that excludes food and energy
prices, these are essential goods that are difficult to substitute away from, and play

a key role in inflation expectations.
On the bright side, the more optimistic lesson is that supply shocks often force
adjustments that prevent a repeat. For instance, back in the 1970s, there was a
recognition that Western economies had become overly dependent on Middle
Eastern oil, and major efforts were made to reduce that dependence. For instance,
the US established the Strategic Petroleum Reserve ( SPR) in 1975, and the
Department of Energy was established in 1977. Moreover, investments were

directed to US energy production, and since 2019, the US has been a net exporter
of energy, rather than a net importer.
Clearly the 1970s are far from an exact parallel. Most obviously, economies are far
less energy intensive today, so oil shocks don’t have as big an impact today.
Nevertheless, the dynamic of repeated supply shocks is one that bears relevance
given how that dynamic is re-echoing today, and with inflation still lingering above
target in many of the world’s biggest economies.

What are the implications for today?
1. Inflation spikes will occur more frequently, as there isn’t the spare economic

### Page 8

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
capacity to meet higher demand.
The first consequence of a supply-constrained world is that unlike much of the
2010s, there isn’t the spare economic capacity (or a negative output gap) that can
easily absorb higher demand. So if there is an unexpected demand shock, for
whatever reason, the consequence will likely be higher inflation rather than higher
output.
Figure 8: IMF: Output Gap in the G7 economies (% of potential GDP)

-4
-3

The 2010s environment of
deficient demand has gone
Source : Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank
This is a particular risk right now because of those longer-term structural shifts on
the supply side. First, global demographics and the retirement of the baby boomers
mean that populations are ageing, and many countries now even have shrinking
working-age populations. This is a big difference from the world of decades past,

when the labour force was generally expanding. Second, the push to reshore
production of key industries has also raised costs, as it requires the duplication of
supply chains. And third, other structural shifts like the push for higher defence
spending have also created an increase in the demand for capital. So there isn't the
spare capacity in the economy to absorb higher demand that there was in the
2010s.
This world of more frequent supply shocks means that central banks must also

trade off how quickly they want to bring inflation back to target against the potential
output volatility that might result. Indeed, we saw this year when the Strait of
Hormuz was blocked, central banks didn’t immediately react with rate hikes, but
they waited a few months to see how the consequences developed. But of course,
that has also meant inflation has stayed above target in many countries, often for
several years. In fact, US PCE inflation has been above 2% since March 2021.

2. Monetary stimulus becomes less effective, as it can’t deal with the direct cause
of supply shocks.
Monetary policy has a complex transmission channel, but when central banks cut
rates, the aim is usually to stimulate demand. This is a very useful tool in demand-
driven recessions like 2008, but it becomes more problematic in the face of supply
shocks.
The problem is that when a supply shock hits, whether that’s a natural disaster, or
the blockage of a key trade route, the economy’s productive capacity drops.

### Page 9

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
Monetary policy cannot affect that directly.
This matters because negative supply shocks have the unfortunate effect of raising
inflation and lowering growth simultaneously. So it puts central banks in a very
tricky position, as they found in the inflationary environment of the 1970s after the
oil shocks. With hindsight, many central banks were too dovish after the first oil
shock and inflation got out of control. In the case of the Federal Reserve, that then

led to a far more hawkish response to the second oil shock, when Paul Volcker
tightened monetary policy significantly to get inflation under control again.
These problems are further exacerbated because central banks operate with
uncertainty about the future. Just as we don’t know today when the Strait of
Hormuz might return to normal, central bankers in 1973 were unsure how long the
oil embargo would last. But because central banks make policy that responds with

a lag, there is inherently the risk of overshooting. If they expect a supply shock to be
permanent, yet it proves temporary, then they risk overtightening. And similarly in
reverse, if disruption is expected to be temporary, but proves permanent, they risk
not doing enough and letting inflation and inflation expectations rise too much.
3. Fiscal stimulus becomes much more likely to cause overheating and crowding
out.
In a supply-constrained world where the economy lacks spare capacity, fiscal

stimulus is more likely to overheat the economy and generate inflation.
This is a big shift from the post-GFC world. Back then, unemployment was high and
deficient demand meant additional government spending didn’t face the same
competition from private investment, so the multipliers from that stimulus were
much higher. Moreover, several countries were able to borrow at incredibly low
interest rates, even negative in some cases.
But this shifted with the pandemic. Not only was the stimulus in 2020 far bigger, but

unlike 2008, the economy then saw a huge wave of pent-up demand once the
pandemic restrictions were lifted, supported by the excess savings accumulated
during the lockdowns. In turn, that contributed to a huge wave of inflation, even
before we then had the additional energy and food price shocks in 2022.
Meanwhile, government bond yields have also returned to their pre-GFC levels,
making the cost of any stimulus far more expensive. And that comes as we are also

seeing a kind of "reverse crowding out", thanks to borrowing from the AI
hyperscalers that is adding to pressure on rates. So in this environment, any fiscal
stimulus will come at a higher cost.
Conclusion: Looking forward
The transition to a supply-driven world means that many of the old economic
assumptions from the 2010s are no longer valid. There’s been a structural break
that means classic demand-side stimulus is not as effective. After years when it was

almost unquestioned that the supply side of the economy would scale up to meet
higher demand, we are now back in a world where supply is a meaningful constraint
again.
As such, the emphasis will naturally shift towards expanding the supply-side of the

### Page 10

14 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 10 Deutsche Bank AG
economy, something we’re already beginning to see. This could be encouraging
more energy production, more housing, or bringing new participants into the
labour force. In essence, what’s needed are policies that raise the productive
potential of the economy, and boost the rate at which the economy can grow
without generating inflationary pressures.
These supply shocks in recent years have been a challenge. Even if they might be

temporary in isolation, the cumulative effect has been to keep inflation elevated,
and we saw that happen back in the 1970s too. This is particularly problematic if the
starting point is above-target inflation, as further supply shocks risk unanchoring
inflation expectations.
Nevertheless, one consequence of a world of more frequent supply shocks is that
actions are then taken to reduce vulnerabilities over the years ahead. For instance,
the 1970s oil shocks led to the widespread realisation that the US needed to

become less energy intensive and produce more of its own energy. As a result, the
Strategic Petroleum Reserve was set up, and the subsequent years saw the US
eventually become a net energy exporter. Similarly today, the closure of the Strait
of Hormuz has forced other countries to secure alternative supply routes and use
strategic reserves. So even before the longer-term geopolitical outcome is clear, we
have seen diversification away from one particular chokepoint. In a supply-driven

world, the incentives will shift towards making those supply chains more resilient,
and better able to withstand future shocks.

《 【粤开宏观】城投化债 的 成效与挑战
——2025 年 城 投 年 报 观 察 （ 一 ） 》
2026-09-06 
《 【粤开宏观】 本轮新型政策性金融工具的
支持方向》2026-09-04

摘要 
当前宏观经济形势可以概括为八个字：总量承压、结构分化。8 月经济数据
释放出一个较为鲜明的信号：经济运行并非同步回升或回落，而是在不同部
门、不同市场之间呈现出更强的结构性分化，表现为工业生产较好与服务业
回落的分化以及房地产总体下行时的内部三重分化。 
其一，工业生产改善，得益于出口较好、新兴制造保持较强韧性。其二，服
务业和传统内需行业偏弱，源自消费、投资和房地产相关需求仍然偏弱。其
三，房地产市场总体继续下行的背景下内部出现新变化和分化，一线城市与
低能级城市、 新房与二手房、 改善型住房与普通住宅之间的表现进一步分化。
经济增速换挡、动能转换的背景下，结构性分化正成为观察当前经济运行的
一条重要主线。 
1、工业与服务业的分化，是当前“供强需弱”格局最直接的体现。8 月规模
以上工业增加值同比增长 5.2%，较 7 月加快 0.7 个百分点，主要得益于出口
较快增长、新兴制造保持较高景气以及采矿业边际修复。电子信息、装备制
造等行业在外需和产业升级带动下表现较强，高技术制造业继续成为工业增
长的重要支撑。 
相比之下，服务业生产指数同比增长 4.1%，较 7 月回落 0.2 个百分点。信息
软件、租赁商务、交通物流等现代服务业仍保持较快增长，但零售、餐饮、
房地产相关服务受居民收入及消费能力影响有所拖累。 
工业和服务业的分化，核心在于工业可以通过出口、企业采购、库存调整等
方式缓冲短期内需波动，而多数服务业更依赖居民和企业的当期消费，因此
在内需偏弱的背景下，工业表现出更强的韧性。 
2、房地产仍处于调整期，但市场内部呈现出更明显的结构性分化。1—8 月
新建商品房销售和房地产开发投资继续下降，销售回款偏弱、房企资产负债
表仍待修复，拿地和新开工意愿不足，使房地产投资短期仍面临较大压力。 
房地产总量承压的同时，房价和交易结构出现三方面变化：一是一线城市率
先出现企稳迹象，而库存较高、人口和需求支撑较弱的低能级城市仍有较大
调整压力；二是新房价格相对稳定，二手房“以价换量”特征更加明显，存
量房交易对整体住房交易的支撑作用上升；三是核心城市改善型、大户型新
房表现相对较强，而二手房定价更多受总价、区位和流动性影响。 
更值得关注的是，预售条件提高、现房销售推进以及房地产融资向项目制和
资金闭环管理转变，意味着行业过去依赖预售回款、高周转和主体信用扩张
的发展模式正在调整，房地产市场也将逐步从高度依赖增量开发转向增量与
存量并重。 
3、展望下一阶段，预计三季度 GDP 增速在 4.4%左右，四季度有望回升至

### Page 2

宏观深度 
请务必阅读最后特别声明与免责条款 www.ykzq.com  2 / 12 
4.6%左右，全年 GDP 增速预计在 4.6%左右，经济运行大体呈现“一季度较
快增长、二季度回落、三季度企稳、四季度温和回升”的走势。 
支撑力量主要来自三个方面：一是出口仍具一定韧性，全球人工智能相关资
本开支以及电子信息、高端设备需求将继续支撑相关制造业；二是高技术制
造、数字经济、新能源和智能装备等新动能仍处于较快扩张阶段，有望继续
成为工业增长的重要来源；三是前期超长期特别国债、中央预算内投资、“两
重”项目及新型政策性金融工具陆续落地，随着资金逐步转化为项目开工和
实物工作量，政策对投资和总需求的支撑有望进一步显现。

一、外需与新动能支撑工业，内需偏弱制约服务业 
8 月，规模以上工业增加值同比增长 5.2%，较 7 月加快 0.7 个百分点；服务业生产
指数同比增长 4.1%，较 7 月回落 0.2 个百分点。消费、投资需求偏弱，对传统工业和服
务业形成制约；出口保持较快增长、新兴制造景气度较高，带动相关行业订单改善，叠
加能源生产边际修复， 共同支撑工业生产回升； 服务业则受到国内需求放缓的明显拖累。 
图表1：8 月规上工业增加值同比回升，服务业生产指数同比回落

资料来源：Wind、粤开证券研究院

（一）消费和投资需求偏弱，传统工业与相关服务业同步承压 
8 月社会消费品零售总额同比增长 0.4%，较 7 月回落 0.2 个百分点。其中，限额以
上单位汽车类零售额同比下降18.5%， 家具类和建筑装潢材料类分别下降7.9%和 11.8%；
家电类增长 2.3%，通讯器材类增长 27.3%。消费增长集中于通讯器材等少数品类，汽车
及住房相关大宗消费仍然低迷。促消费政策效果边际减弱，房地产市场调整以及居民预
期偏谨慎，共同制约耐用品消费。 
投资端延续下行。1—8 月固定资产投资同比下降7.2%， 其中， 制造业投资下降2.3%，
基础设施投资下降 4.0%，房地产开发投资下降 19.9%。销售回款和融资来源收缩继续抑
制房地产投资，企业盈利分化、外部环境不确定以及项目施工受天气影响，可能对制造
业和基建项目推进形成一定影响。 
需求走弱影响相关行业生产。8 月非金属矿物制品业增加值下降 4.1%，水泥、玻璃
等地产和基建链产品继续承压。 服务业方面， 居民消费放缓影响零售、 餐饮和生活服务，
房地产调整压低中介、物业、评估、按揭和设计等活动，企业投资谨慎则减少租赁、招
聘、咨询和市场推广需求。

单指数由 49.6%升至 50.1%，均进入扩张区间； 规模以上工业增加值环比增长0.54%。订

出口需求对相关制造行业的订单和生产形成直接支撑。按美元计价，8 月出口金额

体订单边际改善。

制造业增加值分别增长16.7%和 12.1%； 计算机、 通信和其他电子设备制造业增长17.2%，
电气机械和器材制造业增长 9.9%， 通用设备制造业增长9.8%。 锂离子电池、 工业机器人
和 3D 打印设备产量分别增长 57.2%、34.6%和 29.9%。全球人工智能相关资本开支、国

内数字化改造和新能源产业发展， 持续增加对电子元器件、 电气设备和智能装备的需求。
采矿业增加值同比降幅也明显收窄，由 7 月的 4.2%收窄至 8 月的 1.4%，减少了对
工业总增速的拖累。国际能源市场波动、国内能源保供以及夏季用能需求对煤炭、油气
生产形成一定支撑。8 月煤炭开采和洗选业、石油和天然气开采业出厂价格同比分别上
涨 26.6%和 10.5%， 价格改善也有利于增强相关企业稳产动力。 总体看， 能源保供、 季节
性需求和价格因素可能共同推动采矿业生产边际改善。
（三）现代服务业保持较快增长，国内需求相关服务有所放缓
8 月信息传输、软件和信息技术服务业生产指数增长 9.6%，较 7 月加快 0.3 个百分
点；租赁和商务服务业增长 9.0%，交通运输、仓储和邮政业增长 4.8%。人工智能应用、
企业数字化转型以及工业和外贸活动，对信息、软件、物流和部分商务服务形成较强支
撑。
但服务业整体增速由 7 月的 4.3%回落至 4.1%， 表明现代服务业高增长尚未完全抵消
其他行业的放缓。1—8 月服务零售额同比增长 4.9%，较前 7 个月的 5.0%略有回落；8

月餐饮收入增长 1.1%，服务业商务活动指数为 49.3。居民就业和收入预期仍偏谨慎，住
户信贷扩张动力较弱，可选服务消费的恢复力度有限。
新房销售偏弱，会减少中介、评估、装修设计等交易相关服务需求；房地产开发投
资收缩，则影响工程设计、咨询、租赁和市场推广等项目相关服务。民间投资下降也意
味着企业扩产、开店和新项目减少，从而抑制部分商务服务需求。信息软件等现代服务
业虽然保持较快增长，但目前尚不足以完全对冲国内需求相关行业放缓的影响。
总体来看，8 月工业增速回升而服务业增速回落，反映内需偏弱背景下不同部门支
撑力量存在明显差异。汽车、家具、建材消费以及房地产和民间投资下降，同时抑制传
统工业和相关服务业；出口保持较快增长、新兴制造景气度较高，带动电子、装备等行
业订单和生产改善，叠加采矿业修复，促进工业生产加快。工业还可以通过出口、企业
采购、库存调整和既有产能利用缓冲短期内需波动，而多数服务产品更依赖居民和企业
的当期消费。 因此， 当前工业回升更多体现结构性韧性， 经济修复的均衡性仍有待提高。

二、 房地产仍处调整期： 房价表现分化， 行业发展模式加快转
变
房地产仍是今年经济的主要拖累项。1—8 月，全国新建商品房销售面积和销售额同
比下降 12.1%和 13.0%，降幅较上年同期扩大 7.4 个和 5.7 个百分点；房地产投资同比下

### Page 6

宏观深度 
请务必阅读最后特别声明与免责条款 www.ykzq.com  6 / 12 
降 19.9%， 降幅较上年同期扩大7.0 个百分点。 销售回落制约房企资金回笼， 进而压低拿
地、开工和投资意愿，房地产市场的负向循环尚未完全打破。 
与此同时，房地产市场也出现一些新的变化。房价同比降幅逐步收窄，一线城市率
先企稳，部分核心城市改善型住房表现相对较好，但多数城市房价仍在下降，市场修复
主要体现为结构性改善。 
更值得关注的是，8 月底商品住房销售和房地产融资制度出现重要调整。预售条件
进一步提高，现房销售加快推进，购房资金监管进一步强化，预售住房个人按揭贷款发
放时点后移，房地产开发融资也更加突出以具体项目为核心的风险管理和资金闭环。上
述变化意味着房地产行业过去较为依赖预售回款、高周转和房企主体信用扩张的发展模
式将逐步调整。 
图表2：房地产销售和投资增速仍较低迷

资料来源：Wind、粤开证券研究院

（一） 分城市能级看： 一线城市率先出现企稳迹象， 低能级城市调
整压力仍大 
8 月， 一线城市新房和二手房价格环比均上涨0.1%， 二线城市分别下降0.1%和 0.3%，
三线城市分别下降 0.2%和 0.3%；同比看，一、二、三线城市新房价格分别下降 0.9%、
2.7%和 4.1%， 二手房价格分别下降2.7%、4.9%和 5.6%。 房价表现与城市能级高度相关，
一线城市已经率先出现环比企稳，二线城市仍处于弱调整阶段，三线城市的价格压力最
为明显。 
这种差异主要来自住房需求基础和库存状况的不同。一线城市和部分强二线城市产
业、 就业和公共服务资源集中， 人口吸引力相对较强， 刚性和改善性需求具有一定支撑，
核心区域住宅又具有稀缺性，因此价格能够率先企稳。部分三线城市则同时面临人口流
入不足、住房存量较大和新增需求有限等问题，市场仍需通过降价消化库存。 
一线城市内部同样存在明显分化。8 月，上海、广州和深圳新房价格分别环比上涨
0.4%、0.1%和 0.2%，北京下降 0.2%；上海和深圳二手房价格分别上涨0.3%和 0.1%，广
州持平，北京下降 0.1%。

其中，上海表现相对较强，改善型住房形成明显支撑。上海 144 平方米以上新房价
格环比上涨 0.9%、 同比上涨5.5%， 三个面积段的二手房价格也全部环比上涨， 显示改善
型新房需求和二手房置换需求均较活跃。北京三个面积段的新房价格均环比下降， 144
平方米以上二手房价格下降 0.5%，价格调整范围相对更广。总体看，上海的相对强势可
能与优质改善项目集中成交以及置换链条较为顺畅有关；北京则可能受到在售项目结构
和二手房议价压力等因素影响。

（二） 分新房和二手房看： 新房降幅相对较小， 二手房“以价换量”
特征更加明显
从 70 个大中城市总体表现看，8 月新房价格环比约下降 0.2%、同比下降 3.3%，二
手房价格环比约下降 0.3%、同比下降 5.1%；新房价格环比上涨或持平的城市有 21 个，
二手房仅有 8 个。
新房价格相对稳定，可能与供应收缩和产品结构升级有关。房企投资和拿地更加谨
慎，新入市项目更多集中在核心城市和较好地段；同时，新房供应明显向高得房率、大
户型和高品质产品倾斜，改善型项目成交占比上升，对价格指数形成支撑。部分新盘还
可以通过装修、车位、渠道补贴和特价房等方式让利，实际优惠不一定全部体现为备案
价格下降。相比之下，二手房价格更加市场化，同一小区房源之间直接竞争，急售房源
降价成交后容易形成新的参考价格，购房者选择范围更广、议价能力也更强，因此二手
房通常比新房更快反映供求变化。
二手房“以价换量”特征较为明显。 1—8 月，全国二手房交易网签面积同比增长

10.6%， 而同期新建商品房销售面积同比下降12.1%。 部分城市二手房在经过持续价格调
整后，性价比有所提升，从而带动刚性和改善性住房需求释放。
（三） 分户型看： 新房中大户型相对坚挺， 二手房价格更多受总价
和流动性影响
从一线城市分面积段数据看，大户型新房总体好于小户型。8 月，上海、北京、广
州和深圳 144 平方米以上新房价格环比分别上涨0.9%、 下降0.1%、 上涨0.2%和上涨 0.3%，
表现均好于各自城市90 平方米以下户型。 其中， 上海144 平方米以上新房同比上涨5.5%；
北京大户型新房虽然仍在下降，但同比降幅只有 0.8%，明显小于两个中小户型面积段。
大户型新房相对坚挺，反映当前核心城市中购买力较强的住房需求主要来自改善型
家庭， 同时也与新增住宅项目更多面向改善客群有关。近年来新房在户型设计、 得房率、
社区环境和建筑标准等方面升级较快，优质改善项目集中成交，进一步强化了部分大户
型新房的价格优势。
二手房市场的定价逻辑则有所不同。8 月份，上海 90 平方米以下、90—144 平方米

和 144 平方米以上二手房价格分别环比上涨 0.3%、0.4%和 0.1%；北京分别下降 0.1%、
持平和下降 0.5%；广州小户型上涨 0.4%，大户型下降 0.3%。
二手房价格更多取决于地段、房龄、总价和转售流动性。普通大户型二手房总价较
高、潜在购买者较少，还可能面临房龄较长、户型落后和交易周期较长等问题，业主急
售时往往需要作出更大幅度让价；小户型虽然改善属性较弱，但总价门槛较低，覆盖的
刚需和租赁需求更广，通常更容易成交。相对而言，新房价格更多受到品质改善需求支
撑，二手房价格则对总价和流动性更为敏感。
（四）展望：房地产投资仍将承压， 住房交易结构继续向存量市场
倾斜
房价方面，一线城市、核心区域和高品质住宅相对更有支撑，但多数城市房价仍处
调整阶段，库存较高的低能级城市和普通存量住房压力更大。二手房市场挂牌竞争较为
充分，部分城市仍需通过降价促进成交，价格表现可能继续弱于新房。未来一段时间，
房价更可能呈现同比降幅逐步收窄、环比低位波动、城市和产品分化延续的特征，局部

### Page 9

宏观深度
请务必阅读最后特别声明与免责条款 www.ykzq.com  9 / 12
价格改善能否持续并向更多城市扩散仍需观察。
销售方面，需要区分新房和二手房走势。居民收入和房价预期仍是影响住房需求恢
复的主要因素，新房销售短期仍面临较大压力。8 月底商品住房销售制度调整提高了预
售条件，并将预售住房个人按揭贷款发放时点后移，可能使部分新项目入市和销售回款
节奏放缓，短期对新房销售形成一定扰动。不过，新规对存量项目设置了过渡安排，同
时加强开发融资支持，因此整体影响预计将渐进显现。
与此同时，二手房交易已经成为稳定房地产总交易量的重要力量。当前二手房网签
面积保持同比增长，反映居民住房需求并未完全消失，而是更多转向总价更低、区位和
配套更成熟、价格调整更加充分的存量住房。未来房地产销售端可能继续呈现“新房偏
弱、二手房相对活跃”的结构特征，房地产市场也将进一步由过去高度依赖增量开发的
模式向增量与存量并重转变。
中长期看，提高预售门槛和推进现房销售有助于降低交付风险、改善住房品质和交
易安全，但能否转化为新房销售持续改善，仍主要取决于居民购房意愿和房价预期的变

化。与此同时，存量房交易占比提高，也意味着房地产产业链的重心将逐步发生变化：
与大规模新开发直接相关的土地、建材和施工需求趋弱，而经纪、装修、社区服务、住
房更新以及与改善型置换相关的服务需求重要性上升。
投资方面，短期下行压力仍然较大，改善可能滞后于住房交易。当前新房销售回款
偏弱、房企资产负债表仍需修复，土地购置和新开工意愿不足，是制约开发投资的主要
因素。新的融资制度以项目为中心，通过主办银行制、贷款期限与建设销售周期匹配以
及资金封闭管理等方式，有助于缓解符合条件项目的融资约束，并降低因资金链中断造
成的停工和交付风险。但融资条件改善能否进一步转化为新增投资，仍取决于项目销售
前景和预期收益。尤其在新房需求尚未明显恢复的情况下，房企扩大拿地和新开工的动
力仍可能较弱，因此房地产投资出现趋势性企稳可能仍需一定时间。
三、预计经济三季度企稳，四季度温和回升
上半年 GDP 同比增长 4.7%，其中一季度增长 5.0%，二季度增长 4.3%。结合 7—8
月经济数据，预计三季度经济增速在 4.4%左右， 四季度随着部分政策资金和项目形成更

多实物工作量，增速有望回升至 4.6%左右，全年 GDP 增速预计在 4.6%左右。全年经
济运行大体呈现“一季度较快增长、二季度回落、三季度企稳、四季度温和回升”的走
势。
下阶段经济主要有三方面支撑。一是出口仍有一定韧性。全球人工智能相关资本开
支、电子信息和高端设备需求继续对我国电子、机械和装备制造形成支撑。二是新动能
继续较快成长。高技术制造业、数字经济、人工智能、新能源和智能装备等产业仍处于
较快扩张阶段，相关投资、生产有望继续成为工业增长的重要来源。三是前期宏观政策
形成的实物工作量将逐步体现。超长期特别国债、中央预算内投资、“两重”项目以及
新型政策性金融工具陆续落地，从资金下达到项目开工、施工再到形成投资实物量存在
一定时滞，政策对经济的支撑有望逐步增强。
制约因素仍主要集中在内需。房地产仍是主要拖累项，不仅直接影响开发投资，也
通过居民财富、土地财政和上下游产业链影响消费和企业预期；居民消费和企业投资的
内生动力不足，私人部门自主扩大支出的意愿仍有待增强。
短期看，经济仍将呈现“生产端相对较强、需求端偏弱，新动能较快增长、传统内

需承压”的结构性特征； 中期若政策支撑能够逐步传导至就业、 收入、 消费和企业投资，
经济增速有望进一步提升。

程中恪守独立诚信、 勤勉尽职、 谨慎客观、 公平公正的原则， 独立、 客观地出具本报告， 结论不受任何第三方的授意或影响。
本报告反映了本人的研究观点，不曾因，不因，也将不会因本报告中的具体推荐意见或观点而直接或间接接收到任何形式的
报酬。

与公司有关的信息披露 
粤开证券具备证券投资咨询业务资格，经营证券业务许可证编号：10485001。 
本公司在知晓范围内履行披露义务。

股票投资评级说明 
投资评级分为股票投资评级和行业投资评级。

股票投资评级标准 
报告发布日后的 12 个月内公司股价的涨跌幅度相对同期沪深 300 指数的涨跌幅为基准，投资建议的评级标准为： 
买入：相对大盘涨幅大于 10%； 
增持：相对大盘涨幅在 5%~10%之间； 
持有：相对大盘涨幅在-5%~5%之间； 
减持：相对大盘涨幅小于-5%。

行业投资评级标准 
报告发布日后的 12 个月内行业股票指数的涨跌幅度相对同期沪深 300 指数的涨跌幅为基准，投资建议的评级标准为： 
增持：我们预计未来报告期内，行业整体回报高于基准指数 5%以上； 
中性：我们预计未来报告期内，行业整体回报介于基准指数-5%与 5%之间； 
减持：我们预计未来报告期内，行业整体回报低于基准指数 5%以下。

 【粤开宏观】美国 K 型分化下的加息
逻辑——美联储 9 月议息会议点评 
2026 年 09 月 17 日  投资要点

近期报告

服务业分化，房地产呈现三重分化》
2026-09-15

——2025 年 城 投 年 报 观 察 （ 二 ）》
2026-09-13 
《【粤开宏观】8 月出口增速进一步上升：
“ 新 新 三 样 ” 有 望 打 开 出 口 新 空 间 》

——2025 年 城 投 年 报 观 察 （ 一 ）》
2026-09-06

摘要 
一、美联储重启加息，市场进一步交易“紧缩” 
当地时间 2026 年 9 月 16 日，美联储公布议息会议声明及经济预测，将联邦
基金利率目标区间上调 25 个基点至 3.75-4.00%区间，符合市场预期。本次重
启加息也是 2023 年 7 月以来美联储首次收紧政策。经济预测（SEP）下调失
业率预测、 上调通胀预测， 点阵图中位数显示2026 年仍将加息一次并维持至
2027 年。 沃什沟通上延续克制， 否认这是一次 “市场引导的加息” ， 将加息目
的定义为“取消宽松”而非“增强限制” ，或有意弱化加息对经济和市场的负
面冲击。 
会议后，市场进一步交易“紧缩” ：10 年美债收益率由 4.95%再度回升至 5%
以上，美元指数由 99.7 升至 100.3，国际金价由 4350 美元/盎司下跌至 4265
美元/盎司， 美股三大指数均跳水并收跌。CME 数据显示， 会议后， 市场预计
2026 年 10 月、2027 年 1 月和 4 月美联储将再加息三次。 
二、美国 K 型分化下的加息逻辑 
美联储将本次加息定义为“取消宽松” （remove accommodation） ，并重申原
本的利率水平难以被定义为“限制性” 。在缺少前瞻指引的背景下， 我们需要
思考美联储重启加息的真实考量，更有必要讨论美国政策利率的“合理”水
平，以便进一步研判未来利率路径。 
美联储重启加息，除了沃什不断强调的经济韧性和通胀偏高等因素，还有两
大重要背景：一是地缘局势反复引发油价反弹，国际油价破百，高油价延续
性已超过 2022 年俄乌冲突；二是欧央行、日本央行等已率先加息，市场对美
联储加息的预期升温，美债收益率提前上行，美联储更难“按兵不动” 。 
但是，美国政策利率的“合理”水平（或“中性利率”水平）难以定义，深
层次原因在于美国经济处于 AI 驱动的 K 型分化当中，导致传统经济周期研
判方法失效。 
AI 需求冲击显著支撑当前美国 GDP 增长，但不能显著提振就业。由于美国
GDP 表现与就业市场表现出现较大差异，合理利率水平的判断高度取决于指
标选取。具体来看，根据泰勒规则，我们分别使用美国失业率和实际GDP 测
算产出缺口， 测算得到当前合理的政策利率水平分别为3.88%和 4.09%， 相差
21 个基点。换言之，基于 GDP 表现，美联储可能需要多加息一次。 
当前美国利率水平对于传统部门和多数群体具备“限制性” ，但对于AI 产业
和少数群体不具备； 一个 “折中” 的市场利率还会自动放大K 型分化。 因此，
需要客观认识到货币政策在调控总需求方面的局限性，以及统一利率环境对
于不同经济主体的差异化影响。 
三、未来利率路径与市场影响

### Page 2

宏观深度 
请务必阅读最后特别声明与免责条款 www.ykzq.com  2 / 12 
（一）利率路径：预计年内仍有一次加息，后续路径高度不确定 
基准情形下， 我们预计美联储于2026 年 12 月再加息 25 个基点， 利率区间升
至 4.00%-4.25%。2027 年以后的利率路径取决于多方面因素，主要包括中东
局势与油价走势、AI 资本开支持续性及影响、以及美股会否出现较大级别调
整等。 
（二）市场影响：美债收益率上行风险犹存，美股“AI 牛市”面临更大不确
定性 
美债方面，近期长债收益率上行因素中， 美联储加息预期升温仅是部分原因，
多因素已经导致长债收益率处于上行趋势。未来一段时间，市场可能对于美
联储后续加息节奏保持猜测和担忧，这一不确定性可能抬升期限溢价，导致
长端美债收益率高位运行。 
美股方面，需要进一步关注高利率对“AI 牛市”的潜在冲击。近期美国 AI
安全和风险问题抬头，引发行业监管收紧和产业发展减速的担忧，AI 发展的
乐观预期可能出现阶段性松动。在此背景下，美股“AI 牛市”能否持续免疫
高利率的影响，存在更大不确定性。 
（三）外溢影响：中国经济及金融市场面临一定压力，但总体影响可控 
一是中美利差及资本流动压力，但人民币汇率有望保持韧性。 中国通胀温和
而美国通胀高企，实际利差与名义利差的倒挂程度并不等同；当前中国出口
及资本市场表现总体积极，人民币资产吸引力上升，配置型资金对短期利差
波动的敏感度较低。 
二是外需增长或面临一定风险。若加息导致美国 AI 投资需求阶段性降温， 可
能一定程度上冲击我国 AI 产业链出口。 
三是 A 股可能面临阶段性情绪传染风险，但亦有望走出独立行情。A 股走势
更取决于经济和产业基本面，而非利差压力。中长期看，随着可能的增量政
策出台、稳市资金逐步进场、企业盈利加速兑现，市场有望抵御外部环境冲
击，走出独立行情。

风险提示：美国经济韧性不及预期，美联储独立性不及预期，全球金融市场
波动超预期等。

一、美联储 9 月 FOMC 会议核心看点 
（一）会议声明及经济预测：年内或再加息一次 
当地时间 2026 年 9 月 16 日，美联储公布议息会议声明及经济预测，将联邦基金利
率目标区间上调 25 个基点至 3.75-4.00%区间，符合市场预期。本次利率决议获得 12 位
官员全票通过。 
9 月会议声明继续保持精简，总体对美国经济的表述更加乐观，并有意减少有关地
缘局势及影响的描述。主要改动包括：一是在地缘局势的描述上，7 月份强调地缘局势
的不确定性对经济增长的潜在风险，本次则强调地缘风险下美国消费需求保持韧性，总
体对于美国经济的看法更加乐观。二是，将生产力增长的描述从“有韧性” （robust）改
为“强劲” （strong） ，保留资本开支“有韧性”的提法，总体对于美国生产率的看法更加
乐观。三是，保留“通胀仍高” （Inflation remains elevated ）的措辞，但不再提及“能源
等领域供给冲击”是推升物价的重要因素，暗示通胀成因也来自需求韧性。四是，结尾
新增“本次行动将支持通胀更及时地回落（timelier return） ” ，并重申物价目标。 
9 月经济预测（SEP）下调失业率预测、上调通胀预测，点阵图中位数显示 2026 年
仍将加息一次并维持至 2027 年。相较 6 月预测，增长方面，2026 年实际 GDP 增速小幅
上修 0.1 个百分点至 2.3%，2027 年上修至 2.4%， 反映美联储对经济韧性的信心增强； 就
业方面，2026 年失业率预测下修 0.2 个百分点至 4.1%，且未来四年均维持在这一水平，
说明美联储预计就业市场将企稳且接近充分就业。 通胀方面，2026 年 PCE 通胀率预测中
值为 3.7%、核心 PCE 为 3.4%，均上修 0.1 个百分点，且通胀预计到 2029 年才能回落至
2%目标， 反映美联储预计通胀将较慢回落。 利率预测和点阵图显示，2026 年政策利率预
测中值由 3.8%大幅上修至 4.1%，其中 12 位官员支持年内再加息一次，4 位支持再加息
两次，2 位支持不再加息；2027 年利率预测也上修至 4.1%，暗示 2027 年不降息。
